<p><a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nazianzus" title="Gregory of Nazianzus">Gregory of Nazianzus</a> was born AD 330, <a href="/wiki/Consecration" class="mw-redirect" title="Consecration">consecrated</a> a bishop by his friend <a href="/wiki/Basil_of_Caesarea" title="Basil of Caesarea">Basil of Caesarea</a>; in 372, his father, the Bishop of Nazianzus, induced him to share his charge. In 379, the people of <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a> called him to be their bishop. By the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a>, he is emphatically called "the Theologian".<sup id="cite_ref-contra_arianos_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-contra_arianos-92">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> "What? were not the Apostles strangers amidst the many nations and countries over which they spread themselves? … Peter indeed may have belonged to Judea, but what had Paul in common with the gentiles, Luke with Achaia, Andrew with Epirus, John with Ephesus, Thomas with India, Mark with Italy?"<sup id="cite_ref-20c.42.43_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20c.42.43-93">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint noexcerpt Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:NOTRS" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:NOTRS"><span title="This claim needs references to better sources. (July 2019)">better&#160;source&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ambrose_of_Milan">Ambrose of Milan</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Ambrose of Milan">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/Ambrose_of_Milan" class="mw-redirect" title="Ambrose of Milan">Ambrose of Milan</a> was thoroughly acquainted with the Greek and Latin Classics and had a good deal of information on India and Indians. He speaks of the Gymnosophists of India, the Indian Ocean, the river Ganges etc., a number of times.<sup id="cite_ref-migne_140_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-migne_140-94">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> "This admitted of the Apostles being sent without delay according to the saying of our Lord Jesus… Even those Kingdoms which were shut out by rugged mountains became accessible to them, as India to Thomas, Persia to Matthew..."<sup id="cite_ref-various_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-various-95">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint noexcerpt Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:NOTRS" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:NOTRS"><span title="Do these works even exist? (July 2019)">better&#160;source&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Gregory_of_Tours">Gregory of Tours</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Gregory of Tours">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The testimony of <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Tours" title="Gregory of Tours">Gregory of Tours</a> (died 594): "Thomas the Apostle, according to the narrative of his martyrdom is stated to have suffered in India. His holy remains (corpus), after a long interval of time, were removed to the city of Edessa in Syria and there interred. In that part of India where they first rested, stand a monastery and a church of striking dimensions, elaborately adorned and designed. This Theodore, who had been to the place, narrated to us."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMedlycott190571_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMedlycott190571-96">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Let none read the gospel according to Thomas, for it is the work, not of one of the twelve apostles, but of one of <a href="/wiki/Mani_(prophet)" title="Mani (prophet)">Mani</a>'s three wicked disciples.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><a href="/wiki/Cyril_of_Jerusalem" title="Cyril of Jerusalem">Cyril of Jerusalem</a>, <i>Catechesis</i> V (4th century)</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>In the first two centuries of the Christian era, a number of writings were circulated. It is unclear now why Thomas was seen as an authority for doctrine, although this belief is documented in Gnostic groups as early as the <i><a href="/wiki/Pistis_Sophia" title="Pistis Sophia">Pistis Sophia</a></i>. In that Gnostic work, <a href="/wiki/Mary_Magdalene" title="Mary Magdalene">Mary Magdalene</a> (one of the disciples) says:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Now at this time, my Lord, hear, so that I speak openly, for thou hast said to us "He who has ears to hear, let him hear:" Concerning the word which thou didst say to Philip: "Thou and Thomas and Matthew are the three to whom it has been given… to write every word of the Kingdom of the Light, and to bear witness to them"; hear now that I give the interpretation of these words. It is this which thy light-power once prophesied through Moses: "Through two and three witnesses everything will be established. The three witnesses are Philip and Thomas and Matthew"</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><i>Pistis Sophia</i> 1:43</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>An early, non-Gnostic tradition may lie behind this statement, which also emphasizes the primacy of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew" title="Gospel of Matthew">Gospel of Matthew</a> in its <a href="/wiki/Aramaic" title="Aramaic">Aramaic</a> form, over the other canonical three.
</p><p>Besides the <i><a href="/wiki/Acts_of_Thomas" title="Acts of Thomas">Acts of Thomas</a></i> there was a widely circulated <i><a href="/wiki/Infancy_Gospel_of_Thomas" title="Infancy Gospel of Thomas">Infancy Gospel of Thomas</a></i> probably written in the later 2nd century, and probably also in <a href="/wiki/Syria" title="Syria">Syria</a>, which relates the miraculous events and prodigies of Jesus' boyhood. This is the document which tells for the first time the familiar legend of the twelve sparrows which Jesus, at the age of five, fashioned from clay on the Sabbath day, which took wing and flew away. The earliest manuscript of this work is a 6th-century one in <a href="/wiki/Syriac_language" title="Syriac language">Syriac</a>. This gospel was first referred to by <a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus</a>; Ron Cameron notes: "In his citation, Irenaeus first quotes a non-canonical story that circulated about the childhood of Jesus and then goes directly on to quote a passage from the infancy narrative of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Gospel of Luke</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Since the Infancy Gospel of Thomas records both of these stories, in relative close proximity to one another, it is possible that the <a href="/wiki/Apocryphal" class="mw-redirect" title="Apocryphal">apocryphal</a> writing cited by Irenaeus is, in fact, what is now known as the Infancy Gospel of Thomas. Because of the complexities of the manuscript tradition, however, there is no certainty as to when the stories of the Infancy Gospel of Thomas began to be written down."
</p><p>The best known in modern times of these documents is the "sayings" document that is being called the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Thomas" title="Gospel of Thomas">Gospel of Thomas</a>, a noncanonical work whose date is disputed. The opening line claims it is the work of "Didymos Judas Thomas" – whose identity is unknown. This work was discovered in a <a href="/wiki/Coptic_language" title="Coptic language">Coptic</a> translation in 1945 at the Egyptian village of <a href="/wiki/Nag_Hammadi" title="Nag Hammadi">Nag Hammadi</a>, near the site of the monastery of Chenoboskion. Once the Coptic text was published, scholars recognized that an earlier Greek translation had been published from fragments of <a href="/wiki/Papyrus" title="Papyrus">papyrus</a> found at <a href="/wiki/Oxyrhynchus" title="Oxyrhynchus">Oxyrhynchus</a> in the 1890s.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Saint_Thomas_Cross">Saint Thomas Cross</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Saint Thomas Cross">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Saint_Thomas_Christian_cross" title="Saint Thomas Christian cross">Saint Thomas Christian cross</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Nasrani_cross.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Nasrani_cross.jpg/170px-Nasrani_cross.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="224" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Nasrani_cross.jpg/255px-Nasrani_cross.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Nasrani_cross.jpg/340px-Nasrani_cross.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1117" data-file-height="1473" /></a><figcaption>Saint Thomas Christian cross</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the 16th-century work <i>Jornada</i>, Antonio Gouvea writes of ornate crosses known as <i>Saint Thomas Crosses</i>. It is also known as Nasrani Menorah,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECollins2007119_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins2007119-98">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Persian Cross, or Mar Thoma Sleeva.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAntony2019_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAntony2019-99">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> These crosses are believed to date from the 6th century as per the tradition and are found in a number of churches in Kerala, Mylapore and Goa. <i>Jornada</i> is the oldest known written document to refer to this type of cross as a Saint Thomas Cross. Gouvea also writes about the veneration of the Cross at Cranganore, referring to the cross as "Cross of Christians".
</p><p>There are several interpretations of the Nasrani symbol. The interpretation based on Christian Jewish tradition assumes that its design was based on Jewish <a href="/wiki/Menorah_(Temple)" class="mw-redirect" title="Menorah (Temple)">menorah</a>, an ancient symbol of the Hebrews, which consists of a seven branched lamp stand (candelabra).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECollins2007119_98-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins2007119-98">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> The interpretation based on local culture states that the Cross without the figure of Jesus and with flowery arms symbolizing "joyfulness" points to the resurrection theology of <a href="/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle" title="Paul the Apostle">Paul the Apostle</a>; the Holy Spirit on the top represents the role of Holy Spirit in the resurrection of Jesus Christ. The lotus symbolizing Buddhism and the Cross over it shows that Christianity was established in the land of Buddha. The three steps indicate Calvary and the rivulets, channels of Grace flowing from the Cross.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThadikatt2004114_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThadikatt2004114-100">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_Islam">In Islam</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: In Islam">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Qur%E2%80%99an" class="mw-redirect" title="Qur’an">Qur’anic</a> account of the <a href="/wiki/Disciple_(Christianity)" title="Disciple (Christianity)">disciples</a> of <a href="/wiki/Jesus_in_Islam" title="Jesus in Islam">Jesus</a> does not include their names, numbers, or any detailed accounts of their lives. Muslim <a href="/wiki/Exegesis" title="Exegesis">exegesis</a>, however, more or less agrees with the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a> list and says that the disciples included <a href="/wiki/Peter_in_Islam" title="Peter in Islam">Peter</a>, <a href="/wiki/Philip_the_Apostle" title="Philip the Apostle">Philip</a>, Thomas, <a href="/wiki/Bartholomew_the_Apostle" title="Bartholomew the Apostle">Bartholomew</a>, <a href="/wiki/Matthew_the_Apostle" title="Matthew the Apostle">Matthew</a>, <a href="/wiki/Andrew_the_Apostle" title="Andrew the Apostle">Andrew</a>, <a href="/wiki/James,_son_of_Zebedee" class="mw-redirect" title="James, son of Zebedee">James</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jude_the_Apostle" title="Jude the Apostle">Jude</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_the_Apostle" title="John the Apostle">John</a>, <a href="/wiki/James,_son_of_Alphaeus" title="James, son of Alphaeus">James, son of Alphaeus</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Simon_the_Zealot" title="Simon the Zealot">Simon the Zealot</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENoegelWheeler200286_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoegelWheeler200286-101">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Major_shrine">Major shrine</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Major shrine">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Santhome_Church">Santhome Church</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Santhome Church">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:SANTHOME_CATHEDRAL.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/SANTHOME_CATHEDRAL.jpg/170px-SANTHOME_CATHEDRAL.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="255" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/SANTHOME_CATHEDRAL.jpg/255px-SANTHOME_CATHEDRAL.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/SANTHOME_CATHEDRAL.jpg/340px-SANTHOME_CATHEDRAL.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3600" data-file-height="5400" /></a><figcaption>San Thome Church, built in 1523</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Santhome_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Santhome Church">Santhome Church</a> is said to be the tomb of Saint Thomas in <a href="/wiki/Chennai" title="Chennai">Chennai</a>, India.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> It was built in 1523 by Portuguese missionaries. It is a <a href="/wiki/National_shrine" title="National shrine">national shrine</a>, <a href="/wiki/Basilica" title="Basilica">basilica</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cathedral" title="Cathedral">cathedral</a>. It is an important site for Christians and a major shrine of Saint Thomas.
</p>
